\(R\) — 2 response features per (participant, item): baseline, endline
\(M\) (in token) 2 item metadata (type, direction) appended to every participant token
\(q_\tau\) — shared participant encoder the §9.2 inner DeepSet; no item-level counterpart
\(\hat K\)-row in token dropped cross-item borrowing is now done by attention over learned embeddings, not a hand statistic
\(S\) 4000 200 draws per interim at deploy (heavier per-draw forward pass)
\(E\) 32 32 embedding dim, unchanged

Reused / trained / adapted (cf. §14.1.1):

piece status detail
network classes (\(q_{\text{input}}, q_{\text{keys}}, q_{\text{query}}, q_\psi\)) reused from §13.2 nested DeepSets + item self-attention
network weights trained from scratch ~18k parameters, multi-quantile pinball loss
per-item token removed replaced by learned pool of raw responses (§14.4.3–14.4.4)
aux \(a\) extended \((1/\sqrt n, 1/\sqrt m, n/N, m/N)\), \(N = 503\) — absolute-scale precision added so contraction is representable at any cohort size (§14.4)
input scale same rule raw \((y - 1)/(K_j - 1) \in [0, 1]\), \(K_j\) from dit[cat_length]
target same \(\rho^{(j^*, s)} = r_{j^*}(\theta^{(s)})\), clipped \(\pm 20\)
target scale same as §14.1 per-item standardisation \(\rho_{j^*}/\hat\sigma_{j^*}\); \(\hat\sigma_j=\operatorname{sd}_s\!\big[r_j(\theta^{(s)})\big]\) is the prior-predictive standard deviation of the effect, estimated once on a \(2{,}000\)-sample prior pilot — one fixed constant per item, constant across interims (stored as item_std); the head quantiles are rescaled by \(\hat\sigma_j\) at deployment

14.4.2 Training data and target

The network is trained on the prior predictive of the partial-credit model (§8): every training example is a fresh draw from the model prior, so no SVI fit enters training (6000 steps, batch \(B=24\), fresh draws each step).

Prior. Each draw uses the standard partial-credit-model priors — the same specification fit in Pyro/Stan — with the ordered-category thresholds given a deliberately wide variance: item time-effects \(\beta_t\sim N(0,1)\) for \(t\in\{\text{baseline},\text{endline}\}\); thresholds \(\tau_{j,t,k}\sim N(0,3.5^2)\); discriminations \(\lambda_{j,t}=\lvert T_3\rvert\) (half-Student-\(t\), 3 df, the first item-time fixed to \(1\) for identification); and participant abilities \(\theta_i\sim N(0,1)\). The wide threshold prior is deliberate, for two reasons. First, the training prior must envelope any posterior that can arise at deployment: because the amortiser is only ever fitted where the prior predictive puts mass, the prior predictive has to cover the region the real data’s posterior occupies, or the network is evaluated out of distribution — the prior/deployment mislocation of §14.4.5 is precisely this distribution shift, and a proposal that fails to envelope it cannot be repaired by the head fine-tune. Second, a broad proposal leaves posterior uncertainty for a finite cohort to resolve, which is what makes contraction with cohort size learnable.

Cohort sampling. For each parameter draw, two cohort sizes are chosen independently — the observed size \(n\) and the future size \(m\) — spread log-uniformly over \(\{2,\dots,N\}\) with \(N=503\), so all cohort sizes are represented across a batch. A cohort of \(n_{\text{ab}}\) participants is then simulated: draw \(n_{\text{ab}}\) abilities \(\theta_i\sim N(0,1)\), and at the drawn \((\beta,\tau,\lambda)\) sample each participant’s ordered-categorical response at baseline and at endline from the PCM (inverse-CDF from the cumulative category probabilities), rescaled to \([0,1]\) as \(y/(K_j-1)\). This yields the observed cohort \(x^{(s)}\) and the future cohort \(z^{(s)}\) as raw per-participant response tensors.

Target. The estimand the amortiser fits is the conditional posterior \(p(\rho_j\mid x,z)\) — the posterior over the population effect size given the observed and future cohorts. It is fitted by the standard simulation-based-inference argument (§14.1.7): for a joint draw \((\theta^{(s)},x^{(s)},z^{(s)})\) — parameters from the prior, cohorts simulated from them — the value \(\rho_j^{(s)}=r_j(\theta^{(s)})\) is a single valid draw from \(p(\rho_j\mid x^{(s)},z^{(s)})\), so training the amortiser’s quantiles against \(\rho_j^{(s)}\) (interval score) fits that conditional. Concretely: every amortiser output \(\hat\rho_j^{(s)}\) is scored against the \(\rho_j^{(s)}\) from which its own inputs \(x^{(s)},z^{(s)}\) were generated.

The functional \(r_j(\theta)\) of the drawn parameters \(\theta=(\beta,\tau,\lambda)\) is the direction-aware relative change in the mean item response level between baseline and endline, \[r_j(\theta)=s_j\Big(\bar w_{e,j}/\bar w_{b,j}-1\Big),\qquad \bar w_{t,j}=\mathbb E_{\theta_i\sim N(0,1)}\!\big[\,g_j(\text{response}_{i,j,t}\mid\theta)\,\big],\] where \(g_j\) is the expected score \(\sum_k k\,p_{jk}\) for out-of-7 items and the caseness probability \(\Pr(y_j\ge c)\) for categorical items, \(s_j=\pm1\) the item direction; clipped to \(\pm20\). There is no closed-form expression for \(r_j(\theta)\): the mean response level \(\bar w_{t,j}\) is the expectation over the ability distribution \(\theta_i\sim N(0,1)\) of a nonlinear category probability (a softmax of cumulative threshold sums), and this Gaussian integral has no analytic solution under the partial-credit likelihood. We therefore estimate \(\bar w_{t,j}\) by a reference simulation of \(N_{\text{ref}}=2000\) abilities drawn from \(N(0,1)\). This keeps the construction model-general — it needs only the ability to simulate the model, never to integrate it — under the assumption that \(N_{\text{ref}}\) is large enough that the Monte-Carlo error in \(\bar w_{t,j}\) is negligible against the posterior width, i.e. \(r_j(\theta)\) is treated as the exact target.

Because the target is this population functional while the network sees only finite cohorts, the predictive \(\hat p(\rho_j\mid x,z)\) must contract toward \(r_j(\theta)\) as the cohorts grow — the signal quantified in §14.4.7 and exploited in §14.4.7. The sampler (draw_params, theta_level_rho, sim_cohort in the explore script; python/model_pcm.py) fans each draw out to \(Q=4\) queried items.

14.4.3 Ragged per-participant data structure

The two cohorts enter the network as raw per-participant response tensors, not pooled summaries; because the cohort sizes vary from one training example to the next, they are stored ragged — no padding to a common size, and no participant mask.

Structure. Across a batch of \(B\) examples, all observed-cohort participants are concatenated along a single flat axis, \(x_{\text{flat}}\in\mathbb R^{T\times J\times 2}\), where \(T=\sum_b n_b\) is the total participant count over the batch, \(J\) the items, and the last axis the (baseline, endline) responses; a companion segment vector \(x_{\text{seg}}\in\{0,\dots,B-1\}^{T}\) records which example each participant belongs to. The future cohorts are stored identically as \((z_{\text{flat}}, z_{\text{seg}})\). The arrays therefore hold exactly the real participants — there is no \(N\)-padding and no \(0/1\) mask.

14.4.4 Encoder: pooling, item self-attention, quantile head, loss

The encoder has two MLP stages with a non-parametric mean-pool inside the first — an item-summary DeepSet \(q_{\text{input}}\) (embeds participants, pools them away) and key/query/value encoders \(q_{\text{keys}}, q_{\text{query}}, q_{\text{values}}\) (fuse the two cohort embeddings) — then the §14.1.4 item self-attention and head. Keep two distinct 2-fold axes apart: the group slots (baseline/endline, or the two arms) are the \(R = 2\) response axis inside each participant’s input, fused by \(q_{\text{input}}\); the cohorts (interim \(x\) vs future \(z^{s}\)) are pooled separately and concatenated, which is where the \(2E\) below comes from.

Tokens (inputs to \(q_{\text{input}}\)). Write the raw per-participant responses as \(t^{j}_{i}(x) = y_{i,j}\) for interim participants \(i \le n\) and \(t^{j,s}_{i}(z) = z^{s}_{i,j}\) for future participants \(i = n+1, \dots, N\) (draw \(s\)); each is item \(j\)’s response vector with \(R = 2\) group slots (baseline/endline, or the two arms). These raw inputs are the only objects we call tokens.

Stage 1 — item embeddings (the DeepSet \(q_{\text{input}}\)). For each item \(j\), \(q_{\text{input}}\) (params \(\tau_{\text{input}}\)) embeds every participant’s token — augmented by the item metadata \(M_j = (c_j, d_j)\) — and takes the mean over participants, one embedding per item per cohort: \[e^{j}(x) \;=\; \frac1n \sum_{i=1}^{n} q_{\text{input}}\big(t^{j}_{i}(x),\, M_j;\, \tau_{\text{input}}\big) \in \mathbb{R}^{E}, \qquad e^{j,s}(z) \;=\; \frac1m \sum_{i=n+1}^{N} q_{\text{input}}\big(t^{j,s}_{i}(z),\, M_j;\, \tau_{\text{input}}\big) \in \mathbb{R}^{E},\] with \(m = N - n\) the future-cohort size and the tokens \(t^{j}_{i} \in \mathbb{R}^{R}\) the input features (not a pooled axis). \(e^{j}(x)\) is \(s\)-invariant (\(x\) is one interim cohort shared across all \(S\) draws); \(e^{j,s}(z)\) is per draw. Dividing by the true \(n\) (resp. \(m\)) makes each a sample mean at the actual cohort size — the amortised replacement for the hand group-means of §14.1.3, the standard exchangeable-set summary for variable dataset sizes (Radev et al. 2020; Chan et al. 2018). Internally \(q_{\text{input}}\) embeds \(J(n + m S)\) per-participant tokens in all (\(nJ\) from \(x\), \(mJS\) from \(z\)), which the means reduce to \(J\) embeddings \(e^{j}(x)\) and \(JS\) embeddings \(e^{j,s}(z)\).

Stage 2 — item embedding, keys/query, item self-attention, head. The item embedding concatenates the two cohort embeddings (interim and future — this \(2E\) is the \((x, z)\) split, not the group slots): \[e^{j,s} \;=\; \big[\, e^{j}(x),\; e^{j,s}(z) \,\big] \in \mathbb{R}^{2E}, \qquad JS \text{ such vectors (one per item } j, \text{ draw } s).\] Two MLPs map it to keys and queries in \(\mathbb{R}^{E}\) (input \(2E\), output \(E\)): \[k^{j,s} \;=\; q_{\text{keys}}\big(e^{j,s};\, \tau_{\text{keys}}\big) \in \mathbb{R}^{E}, \qquad q^{j,s} \;=\; q_{\text{query}}\big(e^{j,s};\, \tau_{\text{query}}\big) \in \mathbb{R}^{E},\] both independent reads of the item embedding \(e^{j,s}\), as in §14.1.4 (the variant that instead queries the key, \(q_{\text{query}}(k^{j,s})\), is kept only behind a flag, §14.5.2). The values are a separate projection \(v^{j,s} = q_{\text{values}}(e^{j,s}) \in \mathbb{R}^{E}\) (§14.5.3 default; the legacy tied form \(v^{j,s} = k^{j,s}\) sits behind separate_values=False). Self-attention of query \(j^*\) over the \(J\) item keys of draw \(s\), then the multi-quantile head: \[h^{j^*, s} = \sum_{j=1}^{J} \omega^{j^*, s}_j\, v^{j, s},\quad \omega^{j^*, s}_j = \operatorname{softmax}_{j'}\big( \langle q^{j^*, s}, k^{j', s}\rangle / \sqrt{E} \big)\big|_{j'=j},\qquad \hat\rho_{j^* q s} = \big[\,q_\psi\big(\operatorname{concat}(h^{j^*, s},\, k^{j^*, s},\, a)\big)\big]_q,\ \ q=1,\dots,K.\] (We index the deployed quantiles with all suffixes at the bottom — item \(j\), quantile level \(q\), future draw \(s\) — so the superscript is free for a calibration/head tag: \(\hat\rho_{jqs}\) raw, \(\hat\rho^{\text{cal}}_{jqs}\) after §14.4.8, \(\hat\rho^{\mathrm{Gauss}}_{jqs}\) for a parametric head.) The head input differs from §14.1.4 in both the skip and the aux. There is no raw \(F\)-dim token here, so the skip is the queried item’s key \(k^{j^*, s} \in \mathbb{R}^{E}\) (it plays §14.1.4’s “undiluted own-item signal” role that the \(F\)-dim token \(t_{j^*}\) played there); and the aux is \(a = (1/\sqrt n, 1/\sqrt m, n/N, m/N)\) with \(A = 4\) — the deep-set adds the absolute-scale \(1/\sqrt n, 1/\sqrt m\) (so contraction is representable at any cohort size, §14.4.1), against §14.1.4’s \(A = 2\), \((n/N, m/N)\). Head-input dimension \(E + E + A = 32 + 32 + 4 = 68\) (vs §14.1.4’s \(E + F + A = 42\)), mapped \(q_\psi: 68 \to 64 \to 64 \to K\). (An optional head_raw channel can inject the queried item’s raw pooled \((w_b, w_e, \text{ratio})\) at the head — the nearest re-addition of a raw per-item summary — but the plain deep-set leaves it off.) Learned parameters \((\tau_{\text{input}}, \tau_{\text{keys}}, \tau_{\text{query}}, \tau_{\text{values}}, \psi) \approx 20{,}800\) in total (the separate value MLP \(\tau_{\text{values}}\) adds \(\sim 2{,}400\), §14.5.3).

Counts. \(J(n + m S)\) per-participant tokens \(\to\) pooled to \(J + JS\) item embeddings \(\to\) \(JS\) item embeddings \(e^{j,s}\) \(\to\) \(JS\) keys \(k^{j,s}\) (and values \(v^{j,s}\)) and \(JS\) queries \(q^{j,s}\) (all \(\in \mathbb{R}^{E}\)) \(\to\) \(JS\) outputs \(h^{j^*, s} \in \mathbb{R}^{E}\) (value dimension \(d_v = E\)). The \(J\) keys of a draw are shared across all queried items (the \(j^*\)-dependent empirical \(\hat K\)-row of §14.1 is dropped, §14.4.1), so \(JS\) distinct keys/values here versus \(J^2 S\) in §14.1, while the item self-attention still forms \(J^2 S\) scores.

Block structure / masking. Because the keys are now shared across queries within a draw (not \(j^*\)-dependent), the attention is a square masked operation over the \(JS\) keys — contrast the rectangular \(JS \times J^2 S\) selector of §14.1.4. Stack \(Q = [q^{j^*, s}] \in \mathbb{R}^{JS \times E}\), \(K = [k^{j, s}] \in \mathbb{R}^{JS \times E}\) and \(V = [v^{j, s}] \in \mathbb{R}^{JS \times E}\) (with \(v^{j,s} = q_{\text{values}}(e^{j,s})\) by default, or tied \(V = K\) in the legacy form, §14.5.3); the weights and output are \[\Omega = \operatorname{softmax}\!\Big( \tfrac{Q K^\top}{\sqrt{E}} + \log M \Big), \qquad H = \Omega\, V \in \mathbb{R}^{JS \times E}, \qquad M = I_S \otimes \mathbf{1}_{J \times J} \in \{0, 1\}^{JS \times JS},\] i.e. query \((j^*, s)\) attends to key \((j, s')\) iff \(s' = s\): a block-diagonal mask with \(S\) all-ones \(J \times J\) blocks, one per draw. Within a draw it is the full \(J \times J\) self-attention (every item queries every item, \(J^2\) scores); across draws nothing mixes, so \(J^2 S\) scores in all.

Permutation. The per-item key \(k^{j, s}\) (and value \(v^{j, s}\), query \(q^{j, s}\)) is pointwise — it reads only its own \((j, s)\) item embedding — so it is equivariant to any permutation of the \((j, s)\) grid (permuting the inputs permutes the \(k\)’s identically). The constraint enters at the attention: the output \(h^{j^*, s}\) is invariant to permuting the attended item set (the keys/values in the sum, within a draw) and equivariant over the query item \(j^*\) and the draw axis \(s\). The symmetry group that preserves \(M\) is the within-draw \((S_J)^{S} \rtimes S_S\) (permute items inside each draw-block, permute the blocks), not the full \(S_{JS}\); \(M\) is exactly the object that encodes it. The draws are genuinely exchangeable (i.i.d. posterior-predictive, §14.4.2); the item symmetry is the deep-set exchangeability of §9.2.

Design note. The query reads the item embedding \(e^{j, s}\) (the concatenated cohort embeddings, dim \(2E\)), matching §14.1.4’s \(q_{\text{query}}(t_{j^*})\): query and key are then independent reads of \(e^{j, s}\) rather than the query sharing the \(q_{\text{keys}}\) representation. The earlier variant that queries the key, \(q^{j, s} = q_{\text{query}}(k^{j, s})\), was trained and tested against this (§14.5.2) and is a calibration wash, so we default to the conceptually cleaner embedding-query; the key-query variant stays reachable behind the query_from_token=False flag (and pre-flag checkpoints load as that, pinned in load_fitted_model).

Loss. The head outputs \(K\) monotone quantiles at fixed levels \(0<\tau_1<\dots<\tau_K<1\). Training minimises the pinball (quantile) score, summed over prior draws \(s\), queried items \(j^*\) and levels \(\tau_k\): \[\mathcal L=\sum_{s}\sum_{j^*}\sum_{k}\ell_{\tau_k}\!\big(\rho_{j^*s}-\hat\rho_{j^* k s}\big),\qquad \ell_\tau(u)=\big(\tau-\mathbf 1\{u<0\}\big)\,u,\] with target \(\rho_{j^*s}=r_{j^*}(\theta^{(s)})\) (item \(j^*\), draw \(s\)) and head output \(\hat\rho_{j^* k s}\) (quantile level \(k\)); equivalently the interval score summed over the symmetric quantile pairs \((\tau_k,1-\tau_k)\). The pinball score at level \(\tau\) is minimised exactly at the \(\tau\)-quantile of the target’s conditional law; since \(\rho_{j^*s}\) is a draw from \(p(\rho\mid x^{(s)},z^{(s)})\) (§14.4.2), the fitted head recovers the quantiles of that conditional posterior.

Alternatives considered. A head that instead fits the marginal \(p(\rho\mid x)\) directly — or an architecture that guarantees the law of total probability \(p(\rho\mid x)=\int p(\rho\mid x,z)\,p(z\mid x)\,dz\) by construction (a normalising-flow or diffusion posterior head) — was considered and dropped. At deployment \(p(\rho\mid x)\) is obtained directly from the SVI fit of the observed cohort because we need this to generate future cohorts; the piece SVI cannot supply is the future cohort \(z\), so the amortiser is needed only for the conditional \(p(\rho\mid x,z)\).

Net module python/amortiser_pps_features_deepsetScompAtt_ragged_qpsi_MLP_loss_multiquantilehead.py; trained network directory …-deepsetScompAtt-plain-260812. The evaluation grid is the 29 weekly SVI fits (py-ukraine-interim-weekly-svi-260811, \(n=48\to500\)), reused with no recomputation. (The fixed-\(N\) padded predecessor and the focused interim-1 proposal are catalogued in §14.10.)

14.4.5 Diagnostics

Four diagnostics probe the raw (pre-calibration) network, forward-passing every SVI draw; all quantities exclude the degenerate item CG-VIO_ph-punish. Each is defined here as a statistic on the amortiser output, with the figure that illustrates it; the numbers themselves appear from §14.4.6 on.

Association with the SVI target. For a joint draw, the SVI posterior draw \(\rho^{(s)}_{\text{SVI-}x}\) from the observed cohort is passed through the amortiser to obtain the conditional median \(\operatorname{med}\hat p(\rho\mid x^{(s)},z^{(s)})\). The diagnostic is the Pearson correlation between the two as a function of cohort size \(n\) — whether the encoder’s set-summary retains the cohort’s signal. It necessarily decays with \(n\): as the SVI posterior tightens onto \(r_j(\theta)\) the spread of targets to track collapses, so a falling correlation is expected, and this diagnostic needs to condition on a particular n band.

Conditional calibration (PIT / SBC). Under the §14.1.7 joint-draw construction \(\rho^{(s)}_{\text{SVI-}x}\) is a valid draw from the conditional \(p(\rho_j\mid x,z^{(s)})\) the amortiser targets, so its probability-integral transform under the amortiser’s predictive, \[u^{(s)}_j \;=\; \hat F_j\big(\rho^{(s)}_{\text{SVI-}x}\mid x,z^{(s)}\big)\ \in[0,1]\] — the rank of the target among the predicted quantiles, linearly interpolated — must be uniform if that conditional predictive is calibrated. The scalar summary is the KS distance of the empirical PIT CDF from uniform, \(\text{PIT–KS}_j=\sup_t\lvert \hat G_j(t)-t\rvert\). Illustrated by the PIT box (…_pit_box_by_item.pdf): per item, precomputed whiskers (10/90% quantiles), box (25/75%) and median line of \(\{u^{(s)}_j\}\) across draws, coloured by the signed deviation of the PIT median from \(\tfrac12\); a calibrated item sits at median \(\tfrac12\) with box edges near \(0.25/0.75\).

Marginal calibration. The marginal predictive is built by the law of total probability over the future cohort: draw \(z^{(s)}\sim p(z\mid x)\) — posterior-predictive future cohorts simulated from the SVI fit of the observed cohort \(x\) — run the amortiser on each to get the conditional \(\hat p(\rho_j\mid x,z^{(s)})\), and pool them, \(\hat p(\rho_j\mid x)=\frac1S\sum_{s=1}^S \hat p(\rho_j\mid x,z^{(s)})\). At a fixed interim this should match the SVI posterior \(p_{\text{SVI}}(\rho_j\mid x)\). The scalar summary is the KS distance between the two CDFs, \(\text{marg–KS}_j=\sup_t\lvert \hat F^{\text{marg}}_j(t)-F^{\text{SVI}}_j(t)\rvert\), pooled over interims. Illustrated by the marginal quantile box (…_contraction_cdf_by_item.pdf, with a …RAGDbase… raw-network companion): \(x\)-axis interim, \(y\)-axis \(\rho\)-quantiles, one box per interim for SVI and one for the amortiser (whiskers 10/90%, box 25/75%, median line), so a systematic location or spread mismatch is read off directly.

Posterior contraction. The predictive should sharpen with cohort size at the SVI rate. The contraction law plot (…_contraction-law_trained-vs-svi.pdf) shows, per item, \(\log\mathrm{SD}(\rho_j\mid x)\) against \(\log n\) for the SVI posterior and for the amortiser, with a fitted power law \(\mathrm{SD}=C_j\,n^{-p_j}\) overlaid. The fit takes \((C_j,p_j)\) by least squares of \(\log\mathrm{SD}\) on \(\log n\) across the weekly SVI grid; on log–log axes \(\log\mathrm{SD}=\log C_j-p_j\log n\) is a straight line of slope \(-p_j\), so the constant relative contraction rate \(\mathrm{d}\log\mathrm{SD}/\mathrm{d}\log n=-p_j\) is exactly that slope. The SVI SDs fall along this line; a well-matched amortiser tracks the same slope, an under-contracting one is shallower. The companion contraction factor plot (…_posterior-contraction-factor.pdf) shows the ratio of amortiser to SVI predictive SD against \(n\) — flat at one when the rates agree, drifting above one and growing with \(n\) when the amortiser under-contracts. This rate is the second target, modelled in §14.4.7.

All figures are produced by python/amortiser_diag_plots.py.

14.4.6 Head fine-tune: expanding window and affine shift

The mislocation is corrected by refitting only the quantile head \(q_\psi\) on the operational SVI, with the encoder frozen, followed by a per-item affine location shift. Two fine-tune schedules are compared against the raw network on the weekly grid (conditional PIT–KS; marginal KS):

head fine-tune (\(+\) affine shift) PIT–KS marg–KS
none (raw network) 0.465 0.441
interim-1 only (…_ftheadi1) 0.144 0.238
expanding \(1..k\) (…_ftheadexpand) 0.069 0.157

What is re-optimised, and the loss. Only the head weights \(\psi\) of \(q_\psi\) are refit; the encoder — and hence every cached head input \(h^{j^*, s}\) (§14.4.4) — is frozen. At interim \(k\) the reference draws \(\{\rho_{jis}\}\) (item \(j\), interim \(i\), draw \(s\)) of the SVI fits of all interims \(i \le k\) are pooled (standardised by the global \(\sigma\)) into \(\mathcal{P}_k\), and \(\psi\) is re-optimised by the multi-quantile pinball (quantile) loss over the \(K = 5\) levels \(\tau \in \{.05, .25, .5, .75, .95\}\): \[\hat\psi_k \;=\; \arg\min_{\psi}\; \frac{1}{K\,|\mathcal{P}_k|}\sum_{(i,s,j)\,\in\,\mathcal{P}_k}\;\sum_{q} \ell_{\tau_q}\!\big(\rho_{jis},\; \hat\rho_{jqs}(\psi)\big),\qquad \ell_\tau(y, u) = \max\!\big(\tau\,(y - u),\; (\tau - 1)(y - u)\big),\] where \(\hat\rho_{jqs}(\psi)=\hat q_{\tau_q}(h^{j,s}_i;\psi)\) is the head-mode quantile read-out of \(q_\psi\) at level \(\tau_q\) on the frozen head input \(h^{j,s}_i\) of interim \(i\) (Adam, lr \(3\times10^{-4}\), \(\sim\!800\) steps, minibatch 2048; \(\approx 0.5\) s per \(k\)). The precision aux enters through \(\text{prec} = n_k^{-1/2}\) (§14.4.4). Because the five quantiles are fit independently — the plain head emits five free monotone quantiles — the refit captures the conditional shape, including skew: no symmetry is imposed, so this is not merely a spread rescale.

Interim-1 fitting is not pursued further. A head fitted at interim 1 alone calibrates the anchor but drifts (PIT–KS \(0.144\), marg–KS \(0.238\) over the 29 interims), because the location bias is interim-heteroscedastic — its magnitude shrinks as \(n\) grows, so one fit cannot serve all cohort sizes. The expanding-window fit — refit \(q_\psi\) on interims \(1..k\) at each \(k\) (cost \(\approx0.5\) s/refit, negligible) — conditions on each interim through the frozen embedding and reaches near-nominal calibration (PIT–KS \(0.069\)).

Post-hoc conformal recalibration fails for the same reason. A static per-item monotone PIT map (distribution calibration, Kuleshov et al. 2018), fitted on interim-1 or on the expanding pool, only partly corrects coverage (PIT–KS \(\approx0.56\)–\(0.59\)) and worsens the marginal KS: a single monotone map cannot absorb an interim-heteroscedastic bias, whereas refitting the head can. Set aside (§14.10).

Affine median-shift (pure location, on by default). The head fine-tune fixes the conditional shape but leaves a per-item location residual — the prior-trained encoder systematically under-predicts every item. This is a second, sequential step applied after the head-ft with \(\hat\psi_k\) held fixed — it does not re-optimise \(\psi\). One scalar per item, computed from the fine-tuned head’s own marginal medians against the same SVI of interims \(1..k\), corrects it, \(\Delta_j(k)=\operatorname{mean}_{i\le k}\big[\operatorname{med}p_{\mathrm{SVI}}(\rho\mid x_i)-\operatorname{med}\hat p_{\hat\psi_k}(\rho\mid x_i)\big]\), added to all quantiles of the fine-tuned predictive as a pure translation \(\hat q_\tau \leftarrow \hat q_\tau + \Delta_j(k)\) — it moves location only, preserving the spread and skew the head-ft fit (RAGD_MEDSHIFT, default on). It lowers PIT–KS \(0.075\to0.069\) and marg–KS \(0.180\to0.157\), and removes the systematic under-prediction visible in the marginal quantile box.

Where skew comes from (there is no separate skew shift). The deployable default is exactly head-ft (pinball) \(+\) affine median-shift (then optionally the §14.4.7 BvM variance correction); there is no post-hoc skew operation. Conditional asymmetry is already carried by the free five-quantile pinball fit above, and the affine step is location-only, so the deployed quantiles can be skewed without one. For endpoints whose target is strongly skewed (fold-changes, rates) the §16.3 extension fits the same head in a monotone-warped target space (RAGD_WARP: log2 for a fold, logit for a rate) and unwarps the deployed quantiles — PPS and KS are invariant under the monotone map — so the natural-unit quantiles are skewed by construction; the Ukraine deep-set deploys with warp=none. A fully skew-parametric head (freeq: median \(+\) independent softplus lower/upper gaps, §16.3) is the alternative when the warp is not wanted.

Direct comparison against the hand-token encoder. The same expanding head-ft \(+\) affine remedy is applied to the §14.1 hand-token encoder — itemScompAtt (item self-attention, §14.2.7) — as a fair baseline for the deepset, on the same weekly-29 grid (ids 2..30, \(n=48\to500\)); all four §14.4.5 figures are produced from the shared amortiser_diag_plots module for a like-for-like read:

encoder (expand head-ft \(+\) affine, weekly-29) PIT–KS marg–KS
deepsetScompAtt (learned pooling) 0.069 0.157
itemScompAtt (hand token, §14.2.7) 0.139 0.119

The deepset wins the conditional PIT–KS by \(\approx2\times\) — the object the PPS decision consumes — while the hand token wins the marginal KS by a smaller margin. marg–KS is secondary, since \(p(\rho\mid x)\) is available from SVI directly (§14.4.5); so for the interim decision the deepset stays preferred, with the hand token the marginally better pure effect-size estimator. The trade-off is taken up in §14.7.1.

Both KS metrics vary with cohort size; the all-interim means above hide an \(n\)-gradient. Splitting the weekly grid into small (\(n\le150\); interims 2–5), mid (\(150<n\le400\); 6–13) and large (\(n>400\); 14–30):

encoder PIT–KS s/m/l marg–KS s/m/l
deepset 0.072 / 0.085 / 0.060 0.111 / 0.144 / 0.172
itemScompAtt 0.150 / 0.168 / 0.122 0.070 / 0.101 / 0.138

marg–KS worsens monotonically with \(n\) for every encoder (deepset \(0.111\to0.172\)), and the deepset is the worse of the two at large \(n\). The cause is under-contraction: the deepset’s marginal 5–95% width relative to the SVI width runs \(0.61\to0.80\to1.58\) across the three bands — narrower than SVI at small \(n\), but \(58\%\) too wide at large \(n\). That is, the amortiser’s marginal SD sits above the SVI best-fit power law (§14.4.7) at large \(n\) and stops contracting where SVI keeps sharpening; this upward departure from the law is precisely the residual the Bernstein–von Mises correction of §14.4.7 removes. Conditional PIT–KS, by contrast, has no strong \(n\)-trend and the deepset leads in every band.

14.4.7 Bernstein–von Mises best-fit contraction law

The best-fit law. Bernstein–von Mises gives the leading order: the posterior SD of a smooth functional contracts as \(\mathrm{SD}(\rho_j\mid x)\approx \sqrt{V_{\rho_j}}\,n^{-1/2}\) with \(V_{\rho_j}=\nabla r^\top \mathcal I^{-1}\nabla r\) the delta-method (sandwich) variance at the semiparametric efficiency bound (Vaart 1998; Kleijn and Vaart 2012). Over the finite \(n\)-range of a trial the effective exponent departs from \(\tfrac12\) for item-specific reasons: \(\rho\) is a ratio \(\bar w_e/\bar w_b\), whose delta-method variance is inflated by denominator uncertainty at small \(n\) (Fieller (Fieller 1954)); finite-\(n\) higher-order terms add curvature; the endpoint integrates over the ability distribution; items near a response boundary identify faster; and the finite-population correction \((1-n/N)\) (Cochran 1977) speeds contraction as \(n\to N\). These corrections do not overturn the leading \(n^{-1/2}\); they bend it, and over a finite window that bend reads as a shifted exponent. Formally, define the effective exponent as the local log–log slope \[p_j(n):=-\,\frac{\mathrm d\log\mathrm{SD}(\rho_j\mid x)}{\mathrm d\log n},\] which is identically \(\tfrac12\) under pure BvM. Collecting the corrections into a sub-leading factor \(g_j(n)\), \(\mathrm{SD}^2(\rho_j\mid x)=V_{\rho_j}\,n^{-1}\,g_j(n)\), gives \(p_j(n)=\tfrac12-\tfrac12\,\mathrm d\log g_j/\mathrm d\log n\) — generally \(\neq\tfrac12\), and drifting only slowly with \(n\). Over the trial’s narrow window (\(n:48\to500\) spans only \(\Delta\log n\approx2.3\), a \(3.2\times\) range in \(\sqrt n\)) this slope is nearly constant, so \(\log\mathrm{SD}\) is nearly linear in \(\log n\): a single-exponent power law \(\mathrm{SD}=C_j\,n^{-p_j}\) with \(p_j\) the window-averaged slope. The exponent is item-specific because \(g_j\) — hence its slope — depends on the item’s \(\mathcal I^{-1}\), \(\nabla r\), boundary proximity and floor. Fitting candidate laws to the weekly SVI SD (\(n=48\to500\), 25 points/item):

form median \(R^2\)
power law \(\mathrm{SD}=C\,n^{-p}\) 0.90
floor \(\sqrt{a^2+b^2/n}\) 0.68
BvM (fixed \(p=\tfrac12\)) 0.65
finite-population \(\sqrt{1/n-1/N}\) \(<0\)

The item-specific power law wins (\(R^2=0.90\)): the exponent \(p_j\) has median \(0.45\) but ranges \(0.26\to1.08\), genuinely heterogeneous rather than the universal \(\tfrac12\). The finite-population form fails because the SD plateaus at a nonzero value; the two-parameter floor \(\sqrt{a^2+b^2/n}\) captures that plateau but fits less well than the free power law. The fitted law \(C_j\,n^{-p_j}\) is the object the rest of this section uses — first to justify a self-consistency correction for the deepset’s large-\(n\) marginal upturn (§14.4.6), then as a parametric head that carries the law into the network.

The self-consistency correction. The large-\(n\) marginal upturn is a finite-\(m\) artefact, removed by a post-hoc reshape pinned to the fitted law — no free constant. The argument is four steps.

1. The marginal and its variance split. At deployment, for item \(j\), the marginal is the mixture over \(S\) posterior-predictive future cohorts \(z^{(s)}\) (each of size \(m=N-n\)), \[p(\rho_j\mid x)=\tfrac1S\sum_{s=1}^S \hat p(\rho_j\mid x,z^{(s)}).\] Write \(\hat\rho_{jqs}\) for the deployed \(q\)-th quantile (\(q=1,\dots,5\) at \(\tau\in\{.05,.25,.5,.75,.95\}\)) of item \(j\)’s \(s\)-th conditional; its median is \(\mu_{js}=\hat\rho_{j3s}\) and Gaussian-equivalent SD \(\sigma_{js}=(\hat\rho_{j5s}-\hat\rho_{j1s})/3.290\). By the law of total variance, \[\operatorname{Var}_{\text{mix}} = \underbrace{\tfrac1S\textstyle\sum_s \sigma_{js}^2}_{W\ (\text{within})} + \underbrace{\tfrac1S\textstyle\sum_s (\mu_{js}-\bar\mu_j)^2}_{B\ (\text{between})},\qquad \bar\mu_j=\tfrac1S\textstyle\sum_s\mu_{js}.\] As \(n\to N\), \(m\to0\): each \(z^{(s)}\) is a high-variance draw, the \(\mu_{js}\) scatter, and the empirical \(B\) inflates — the marginal SD turns up instead of contracting. Finite-\(m\) noise, not signal.

2. The target variances from the law. Fit the item contraction law \(\operatorname{SD}(\rho_j\mid x_{1:n})=C_j\,n^{-p_j}\) to the SVI reference posterior SDs \(\{\operatorname{sd}(\rho_j\mid x_{1:n_k})\}_k\) across the interim grid (OLS of \(\log\operatorname{SD}\) on \(\log n\); the shrink option James–Stein-shrinks \(p_j\) toward the pooled median \(\;w_j=\operatorname{Var}_{\text{btw}}/(\operatorname{Var}_{\text{btw}}+\operatorname{se}_j^2)\), then re-anchors \(C_j\)). Bernstein–von Mises — a posterior variance depends to leading order on the sample size, not its values — pins both targets: the marginal at \(n\), and every conditional at \(n+m=N\), \[T=\big(C_j\,n^{-p_j}\big)^2,\qquad W_{\text{true}}=\big(C_j\,N^{-p_j}\big)^2,\qquad B^\ast=T-W_{\text{true}}=C_j^2\big(n^{-2p_j}-N^{-2p_j}\big)\ge0.\] \(B^\ast=\operatorname{Var}_z(\mathbb E[\rho\mid x,z])\) is the contraction the \(m\) future observations legitimately buy: \(B^\ast\to0\) as \(n\to N\) (no data left to collect) and \(B^\ast\to T\) as \(n\to0\) — the opposite of the empirical \(B\)’s large-\(n\) inflation.

3. The reshape. Scale each conditional’s width by \(\alpha_j\) and shrink the between-draw median spread by \(\beta_j\), giving the calibrated quantile \(\hat\rho^{\text{cal}}_{jqs}\) (item \(j\), level \(q\); per draw \(s\), which we suppress in the symbol), \[\alpha_j=\sqrt{W_{\text{true}}/W},\qquad \beta_j=\sqrt{B^\ast/B},\qquad \hat\rho^{\text{cal}}_{jqs}=\big[\bar\mu_j+\beta_j\,(\mu_{js}-\bar\mu_j)\big]+\alpha_j\,(\hat\rho_{jqs}-\mu_{js}),\] so the corrected mixture variance is \(\alpha_j^2W+\beta_j^2B=W_{\text{true}}+B^\ast=T\) exactly.

4. Properties. A post-hoc per-item pass over the deployed quantiles (RAGD_BVM): no retraining, using only the \(\operatorname{Var}(\rho\mid x)\) fit already in hand. It acts only on the marginal spread — conditional PIT–KS is unchanged — and the shift of §14.4.6 (location) and this reshape (scale) commute.

Parametric heads that carry the law (a different mechanism). The \(\alpha,\beta\) reshape above corrects already-deployed quantiles post-hoc. An alternative bakes the law into the head’s output instead — the head emits the law through the \(\hat\rho^{\mathrm{Gauss}}_{jqs}=m_{js}+z_q\,s_j(n)\) formula below and is trained that way, rather than reshaped afterward; applying the §14.4.7 reshape on top gives the calibrated output \(\hat\rho^{\mathrm{Gauss\text{-}power+cal}}_{jqs}\) (and \(\hat\rho^{\mathrm{Gauss\text{-}floor+cal}}_{jqs}\)), i.e. A \(+\) BvM / C \(+\) BvM. This is intrinsically justified when the head’s width already obeys the law, so the width is made parametric (head_mode) without touching the encoder. Everything up to the §14.4.4 head is unchanged: item self-attention still yields the output \(h_{j^*}\), and the shared MLP \(q_\psi\) still consumes \(\operatorname{concat}(h_{j^*},k_{j^*},a)\) and emits a length-\(K\) vector \(\text{raw}\). What changes is only the interpretation of that vector. In plain mode \(\text{raw}\) is returned as the \(K\) quantiles directly; in the parametric modes its leading entries are read as a few shape parameters, from which the \(K\) quantiles are rebuilt deterministically as a fixed-shape Gaussian predictive whose scale follows the law, \[\hat\rho^{\mathrm{Gauss}}_{jqs} = m_{js} \;+\; z_q\,s_j(n),\qquad z_q=\Phi^{-1}(\tau_q)\in\{-1.64,-0.67,0,0.67,1.64\},\] the standard-normal quantile multipliers \(z_q\) fixed, and the cohort size entering only through the absolute-scale aux precision \(\text{prec}=a_1=n^{-1/2}\) (§14.4.4). The trunk is thus shared; only the last map from \(\text{raw}\) to quantiles is replaced, and the two forms differ solely in the width \(s_j(n)\):

  • A — power law: \(q_\psi\)’s first three outputs give \(m=\text{raw}_0\), \(C_j=\operatorname{softplus}(\text{raw}_1)>0\) and \(p_j=\sigma(\text{raw}_2)\in(0,1)\), so \(s_j(n)=C_j\,n^{-p_j}=C_j\,\text{prec}^{2p_j}\). Location, scale and exponent are all item- and query-conditioned through \(h_{j^*}\), while the \(n\)-dependence is hard-wired to the BvM power law. (Regularised variant B adds a prior \(\lambda\,\mathbb E[(p_j-\tfrac12)^2]\) pulling the exponent toward \(\tfrac12\), since \(p\) is only weakly identified over the trial’s \(\sim3\times\) \(\sqrt n\) range.)
  • C — floor: \(q_\psi\)’s first three outputs give \(m\) and \(a_j,b_j=\operatorname{softplus}(\cdot)>0\), so \(s_j(n)=\sqrt{a_j^2+b_j^2/n}\), encoding the non-zero plateau directly (no exponent).

Because the trunk is untouched, the parametric head is a drop-in: the expanding head fine-tune (§14.4.6) still refits \(q_\psi\), and the affine shift still corrects the emitted location \(m\).

Trained, the heads roughly double the training-distribution contraction slope (probe \(-0.32/-0.35\) vs the plain net’s \(-0.20\)) and the power-law exponent tracks SVI for the strongly-contracting items (\(p_{\text{SVI}}/p_{\text{amo}}\) of \(1.08/0.91\), \(0.99/0.78\)). On their own — expanding head fine-tune \(+\) affine shift, without the correction — they do not beat the plain net on aggregate calibration (A power-law PIT–KS \(0.112\), marg–KS \(0.189\); C floor \(0.115\)/\(0.185\); plain \(0.069\)/\(0.157\)): the low-SD mental-health items (\(p_{\text{SVI}}\approx0.3\)) remain under-learned, and a rigid trained width helps only the strongly-contracting items. Directories …-powerlaw, …-floor and their _ftheadexpand deployments.

Result of the correction. Each trained parametric head (A power-law, C floor) is deployed with and without the post-hoc \(\alpha,\beta\) reshape of step 3 — adding it tracks SVI’s contraction with the large-\(n\) upturn removed (matching fitted slopes per item, e.g. \(-0.005\) vs \(-0.005\)); PIT–KS is unchanged, since the reshape acts only on the marginal. Two axes are crossed: the head (its trained law-following width) and the post-hoc reshape (on/off). The plain net of §14.4.6 — no contraction law at all — is the no-law reference (marg–KS \(0.157\), not a row here). The post-hoc reshape always fits the power law \(C_j n^{-p_j}\) to the SVI SDs, whatever the head.

config (all on head-ft \(+\) affine) marg–KS (\(n\le200\)) (mid) (\(n\ge450\)) (all) (\(n\ge498\))
A — trained Gaussian BvM power law 0.169 0.194 0.193 0.189 0.224
A \(+\) BvM — BvM post-hoc reshape of the power-law head’s \(q_\psi\) output 0.161 0.183 0.180 0.177 0.194
C — trained Gaussian BvM floor 0.154 0.185 0.199 0.185 0.232
C \(+\) BvM — BvM post-hoc reshape of the floor head’s \(q_\psi\) output 0.147 0.171 0.170 0.166 0.231

Notably C \(+\) BvM’s large-\(n\) marg–KS (\(0.170\)) beats the plain net \(+\) affine (\(0.181\)) — the correction supplies exactly the missing large-\(n\) contraction, as a principled step rather than a rigid trained width. Directories …-powerlaw-ftheadexpand-bvm-260828, …-floor-ftheadexpand-bvm-260828. A/C \(+\) BvM is the statistically coherent estimator of §14.0 in the sense that its conditional width is law-parametric by construction.

The reshape is head-agnostic — and helps the plain head most. Because the \(\alpha,\beta\) reshape fits its own power law to the SVI SDs, it applies to any deployed head — including the free-quantile plain head of §14.4.6, whose conditional calibration is the best of the three. On the same weekly-29 grid (…-weekly-svi-260924; plain + affine reproduces the §14.4.6 reference, marg–KS \(0.152\) vs \(0.157\)):

config (plain head, head-ft \(+\) affine) PIT–KS marg–KS (all) (\(n\le200\)) (mid) (\(n\ge450\))
plain \(+\) affine (§14.4.6) 0.071 0.152 0.115 0.141 0.176
plain \(+\) affine \(+\) BvM reshape 0.071 0.132 0.115 0.138 0.136

PIT–KS is unchanged (\(0.071\to0.071\)), confirming empirically that the reshape acts only on the marginal (here \(\alpha\approx1\), so the within-draw perturbation is negligible and only the spurious between-draw spread \(B\) is shrunk), while marg–KS falls \(0.152\to0.132\) — entirely in the large-\(n\) band (\(n\ge450\): \(0.176\to0.136\)) where the finite-\(m\) upturn lives; small-\(n\) is untouched (\(B^\ast\) already small). This is the best-calibrated configuration on this grid: it keeps the plain head’s conditional-PIT lead (0.071, vs the A/C heads’ \(0.11\)–\(0.12\)) and beats A/C \(+\) BvM on marg–KS (\(0.132\) vs \(0.166\)/\(0.177\)). So while A/C carry the law in the conditional width, applying the §14.4.8 single-pass correction to the plain head gives a law-consistent marginal without surrendering the free head’s conditional fit — the recommended deployable estimator, with A/C the parametric alternative when a law-constrained conditional width is itself wanted.

14.4.8 Combining the location and contraction corrections

One pass. The §14.4.6 affine shift (location) and the §14.4.7 BvM reshape (scale) are commuting affine maps, so they collapse into a single per-item deploy-time step producing the calibrated quantile \(\hat\rho^{\text{cal}}_{jqs}\). The shift is a translation \(\hat\rho_{jqs}\mapsto \hat\rho_{jqs}+\Delta_j(k)\); the reshape centres on the per-draw medians \(\mu_{js}=\hat\rho_{j3s}\) and grand mean \(\bar\mu_j\) and rescales — both are translation-invariant, so \(\alpha_j,\beta_j\) do not depend on \(\Delta\) and \(\Delta\) passes straight through: \[\hat\rho^{\text{cal}}_{jqs}=\underbrace{\bar\mu_j+\beta_j\,(\mu_{js}-\bar\mu_j)}_{\text{shrink between-draw spread}}\;+\;\underbrace{\alpha_j\,(\hat\rho_{jqs}-\mu_{js})}_{\text{scale within-draw spread}}\;+\;\underbrace{\Delta_j(k)}_{\text{locate}},\qquad \alpha_j=\sqrt{\tfrac{W_{\text{true}}}{W}},\ \ \beta_j=\sqrt{\tfrac{B^\ast}{B}},\] with \(\Delta_j\), \(\alpha_j\), \(\beta_j\) all read from the same raw head-ft quantiles \(\hat\rho_{jqs}\) — no sequential dependence. This is numerically identical to applying the two steps in order (what the deploy driver does today), and it gracefully covers the BvM-off default (\(\alpha_j=\beta_j=1\Rightarrow \hat\rho^{\text{cal}}_{jqs}=\hat\rho_{jqs}+\Delta_j\)): the whole deployment calibration in one line.

Implemented as combined_calibration (shared amortiser_calibration), deployed on the plain head over the weekly-29 grid against the two-step sequential path: the per-draw quantiles agree to \(\max_k\lvert\cdot\rvert = 2\times10^{-6}\) (float32 noise), so every downstream metric is identical.

calibration of the plain head (head-ft) PIT–KS marg–KS (all) (\(n\le200\)) (mid) (\(n\ge450\))
sequential: affine (§14.4.6) then BvM reshape (§14.4.7) 0.071 0.132 0.115 0.138 0.136
single-pass combined (this section) 0.071 0.132 0.115 0.138 0.136

Hierarchical (Stein) exponent shrinkage — the MVN default. The law fit \((\hat C_j,\hat p_j)\) comes from an OLS of \(\log\operatorname{SD}(\rho_j\mid x_{1:n_k})\) on \(\log n_k\) over only the handful of interims, so the per-item exponent \(\hat p_j\) is high-variance and a noisy item can over-pin the width at large \(n\). When the items are exchangeable — the MVN components are — we borrow strength across them by an empirical-Bayes / James–Stein shrink of the exponent toward the pooled median \(\bar p=\operatorname{median}_j\hat p_j\) before forming \(\alpha_j,\beta_j\): \[\hat p^{\mathrm{JS}}_j=w_j\,\hat p_j+(1-w_j)\,\bar p,\qquad w_j=\frac{v_{\mathrm{btw}}}{v_{\mathrm{btw}}+\operatorname{se}_j^2},\qquad v_{\mathrm{btw}}=\big[\widehat{\operatorname{Var}}_j(\hat p_j)-\overline{\operatorname{se}^2}\big]_+,\] where \(\operatorname{se}_j^2\) is the OLS slope variance of item \(j\)’s fit (residual variance \(/\sum_k(\log n_k-\overline{\log n})^2\)) and \(v_{\mathrm{btw}}\) is the between-item exponent variance with the within-item fit noise removed. The scale constant is re-anchored at the shrunk slope, \(\log\hat C^{\mathrm{JS}}_j=\overline{\log\operatorname{SD}(\rho_j\mid x_{1:n_k})+\hat p^{\mathrm{JS}}_j\log n_k}\) (mean over \(k\)), so the law still passes through the item’s own SDs. The reshape then uses \((\hat C^{\mathrm{JS}}_j,\hat p^{\mathrm{JS}}_j)\) in place of \((\hat C_j,\hat p_j)\) throughout; \(T\), \(W_{\text{true}}\), \(B^\ast\), \(\alpha_j\), \(\beta_j\) and hence \(\hat\rho^{\text{cal}}_{jqs}\) are otherwise exactly as above. The shrink is adaptive and self-calibrating: \(w_j\to1\) for a clean fit (barely moved) and \(w_j\to0\) for a noise-dominated one (pulled to the pool), so pure-noise exponent spread pools fully while genuine heterogeneity is kept — strictly better than hard-pinning \(p_j=\tfrac12\). Enabled by RAGD_BVM_SHRINK (shrink=True in bvm_correction / combined_calibration); it is the MVN benchmark’s default, where the exact \(n^{-1/2}\) law makes the pooled exponent especially informative.

Why not fold it into the fine-tune loss instead? One could add a second term beside the pinball loss of §14.4.6 — fit a per-item location (and scale) jointly with \(\psi\) against the SVI reference — and it would run only at fine-tune time, since the reference exists only at deployment (the prior training stays generic). We do not, for three reasons. (i) \(\Delta_j\) (the median residual) and \((\alpha,\beta)\) (the law-pinned width) are closed-form exact minimisers of their targets; a gradient term can at best match them, while adding weight hyperparameters and optimiser cost. (ii) The corrections are a post-hoc affine map that commutes with and is orthogonal to the conditional-shape fit \(\psi\) — pinball fits \(Q_\tau(\rho\mid x,z)\); the shift and reshape only relocate and rescale it — so there is no cross-term for joint optimisation to exploit. (iii) The scale target \(B^\ast=C_j^2\big(n^{-2p_j}-N^{-2p_j}\big)\) is a theoretical law value pinned at \(N_{\text{ref}}\), not an empirical data moment, so a penalty \((B-B^\ast)^2\) merely drives \(B\to B^\ast\) iteratively — exactly the closed-form \(\beta\), no better, and finite-\(m\)-robust only because the target comes from the law rather than the interim’s own (noisy) variance. The closed-form single-pass map is therefore preferred: exact, hyperparameter-free, one deployment-time line.

14.4.9 Amortising the decision thresholds

The interim decision (§4) carries two thresholds: the effect threshold \(\eta_0\) in \(H_1^{(j)}=\{\rho_j>\eta_0\}\), and the decision threshold \(\eta_H\) in \(\widehat{\text{PPS}}^{(j)}=\frac1S\sum_s\mathbf 1\{\hat P(H_1^{(j)}\mid x,z^{(s)})>\eta_H\}\). Both should be free at deployment, without retraining.

\(\eta_H\) is free by construction — it is only a cutoff applied to the already-computed per-draw success probabilities, so any value is a pure post-processing choice.

\(\eta_0\) enters through the conditional CDF, \(\hat P(H_1\mid x,z^{(s)})=1-\hat F(\eta_0\mid x,z^{(s)})\), so it is free wherever the predictive is accurately resolved at \(\eta_0\) — the question is which predictive representation resolves an arbitrary \(\eta_0\) best, especially in the tails where the \(K=5\) quantile grid interpolates crudely. Four routes (net directories …-deepsetScompAtt-{plain,powerlaw,dense19,eta0amortise}-…):

  1. 5-quantile interpolation (the deployable §14.4.6 head): \(1-\operatorname{interp}(\eta_0)\) on the five deployed quantiles.
  2. Parametric Gaussian (the power-law / floor head of §14.4.7): the head emits \((m,s)\), so \(\hat P(H_1\mid x,z)=\Phi\big((m-\eta_0)/s\big)\) is analytic for any \(\eta_0\), smooth in the tails.
  3. Dense quantiles: retrain the head with \(K=19\) levels and a pinball loss, for finer CDF interpolation.
  4. \(\eta_0\)-conditioned head: retrain with \(\eta_0\) appended to the aux and sampled per example, and replace the quantile head with a success-probability head \(\hat P(H_1\mid x,z,\eta_0)=\sigma\big(q_\psi(\cdot)\big)\) (one output, head_mode='successprob') trained by binary cross-entropy against \(\mathbf 1\{\rho>\eta_0\}\) — a direct amortisation of the decision over \(\eta_0\).

Scoring each route by the calibration error of the marginal success probability against SVI, \(\big\lvert \hat P(\rho>\eta_0\mid x)-P_{\text{SVI}}(\rho>\eta_0\mid x)\big\rvert\), averaged over items and interims across a standardised-threshold sweep (body \(|\eta_0/\hat\sigma_j|\le\tfrac12\), tail \(\ge\tfrac32\)):

route body tail all
1 — 5-quantile interp 0.041 0.030 0.034
2 — parametric Gaussian 0.033 0.030 0.031
3 — dense-19 quantiles 0.047 0.029 0.035
4 — \(\eta_0\)-conditioned 0.053 0.029 0.038

The parametric Gaussian wins: lowest overall error, decisively best at the centre (near the median, \(0.012\) vs \(0.03\)–\(0.08\)), tied elsewhere, and zero retraining — its analytic \(\Phi\) resolves any \(\eta_0\). Denser quantiles do not beat the five-quantile baseline (the predictive is close to Gaussian, so five knots already capture the tails), and the \(\eta_0\)-conditioned head helps only in the far tail while being worst at the centre — its BCE head is sharpest where the success probability saturates and weakest near \(p=\tfrac12\). A full retrain for \(\eta_0\)-conditioning is therefore not justified.

Choosing \(\eta_0\) on a scientific scale. The effect \(\rho_j\) is a per-item ratio whose prior-predictive spread \(\hat\sigma_j\) ranges widely (\(0.86\) to \(5.6\)). A threshold expressed in standardised units — a fixed \(\eta_0/\hat\sigma_j\) across items — is not comparable across items: at \(\eta_0/\hat\sigma_j=1\) a wide item sits at a \(560\%\) change and a narrow one at \(86\%\), so its PPS is degenerate (\(0\) or \(1\)). The remedy is to fix \(\eta_0\) on the raw endpoint-change scale, \(\eta_0\in\{0,25,50,75,100\}\%\), and query each item at its own \(\eta_0/\hat\sigma_j\). This is exact for either representation: for the \(\eta_0\)-conditioned head the standardisation cancels, \(\hat P(\rho_{\text{std}}>\eta_0/\hat\sigma_j\mid x,z)=\hat P(\rho>\eta_0\mid x,z)\), so the raw success probability is recovered whatever fixed \(\hat\sigma_j\) is used. Deployed on the weekly grid the raw-percentage PPS stays interior (not saturated): the mean over items and interims is \(0.75,0.26,0.12,0.06,0.03\) at \(\eta_0=0,25,50,75,100\%\), and the share of (item, interim) cells with a non-degenerate PPS peaks at \(46\%\) at \(\eta_0=25\%\). Diagnostics in …-eta0amortise-260830/: …_pps_by_item_eta0_grid.pdf (PPS by item \(\times\) \(\eta_0\%\)) and …_rho_vs_eta0_lines_by_item.pdf (the SVI \(\rho\)-predictive with the raw-percentage \(\eta_0\) thresholds overlaid).

Summary. \(\eta_H\) is free post-hoc; \(\eta_0\) is free through the predictive CDF, best served by the parametric Gaussian head (analytic, no retraining), with the operational \(\eta_0\) fixed on the raw percentage-change scale.

14.4.10 Amortising over items

Motivation. The §14.4 amortiser is trained for one fixed item set (Ukraine’s \(J=20\)). A single network over a variable item set — \(J=2,\dots,J_{\max}\) items — turns it from a per-trial artefact into a per-family asset, deployable on any partial-credit application (§3). Those applications span \(J\) from \(6\) (the ChatGPT survey) to \(\approx51\) (the IBD PROM battery), with category counts \(K\in\{2,\dots,10\}\) (most \(\{4,5,7\}\)); rounding up we set \(J_{\max}=64\).

Design (no padding on any axis). Items enter fixed-\(J\)-per-batch, \(J\) varied across batches: each step draws one \(J_b\sim U\{2,J_{\max}\}\) and builds dense \((B,J_b,\cdot)\) tensors, so there is no item padding and no mask (JAX recompiles once per distinct \(J_b\)). Participants stay ragged (segment vectors, §14.4.3), and each (participant, item, time) cell holds a single scalar response \(k/(K_j-1)\), so heterogeneous \(K\) never introduces zeros either. Each synthetic item draws a category count \(K\sim\) weighted \(\{2,4,5,7,8,9,10\}\), an endpoint type (expected score or caseness), a direction, and PCM parameters from the §14.4.2 priors, so the network learns to price an item from its features, not its identity. The metadata is \((\text{type},\text{direction},K/K_{\max},c/K_{\max})\) — the caseness threshold \(c\) is the fourth feature; the encoder already reads \(J\) from the metadata shape and a generic \(M\), so no architecture change is needed. The per-item \(\hat\sigma_j\) of §14.4.1 is replaced by a single global \(\hat\sigma\) (pilot \(\approx4.8\)); the power-law head’s \(C_j\) (§14.4.7) learns the residual per-item scale. Two cheap calibration add-ons recover most of what the global \(\hat\sigma\) gives up: a data-driven scale channel — the per-item SD of the endpoint change across the observed cohort, injected at the head via segment_sum — and a raw level/ratio fingerprint (head_raw: baseline and endline means and their ratio at the head). Net directory …-itemamortise-J64-scale-feat-260831 (trained \(\sim1.5\) h).

Item-count invariance holds. Deployed on Ukraine’s \(20\) items without retraining — items the network never saw — its median tracks the SVI target as well as the item-specific net: the association (Pearson \(\rho\) of the amortiser median against the SVI draw) is \(0.91\)–\(0.95\) on the out-of-7 items and \(0.73\)–\(0.76\) on the categorical items at small–mid \(n\), against the item-specific baseline’s \(\approx0.82/0.57\) (§14.4.5); the raw (uncalibrated) PIT–KS is \(0.31\)–\(0.46\), already below the item-specific net’s \(\approx0.60\). Both decay with \(n\) as the SVI posterior tightens, exactly as in §14.4.5.

End-to-end on Ukraine. With the standard expanding head fine-tune \(+\) affine shift (§14.4.6), the item-amortised net reaches PIT–KS \(0.119\) and marg–KS \(0.159\). Against the reference points: the marginal now matches the item-specific plain deepset (\(0.157\)), and the conditional PIT–KS beats the hand-token encoder (\(0.139\), §14.4.6); the item-specific PPS (\(\eta_0=0.5\)) is well defined. The two add-ons each contribute one axis: the scale channel lifts the conditional (PIT–KS \(0.134\to0.119\)), the fingerprints lift the marginal (marg–KS \(0.177\to0.159\)).

Deployed on Ukraine’s 20 items (never seen in training), expanding head-ft \(+\) affine (§14.4.6):

All directories below sit under py-ukraine-interim-amortise-; the suffix column gives the end of each name (results in the _ftheadexpand deploy dir).

Configuration Directory suffix PIT–KS marg–KS
Item-amortised (global \(\hat\sigma\), power-law head) — base deepsetScompAtt-itemamortise-J64-260831 0.134 0.177
\(+\) data-driven scale channel deepsetScompAtt-itemamortise-J64-scale-260831 0.119 0.177
\(+\) raw level/ratio fingerprints (scale-feat, deployed) deepsetScompAtt-itemamortise-J64-scale-feat-260831 0.119 0.159
ref: item-specific plain deepset (bespoke, §14.4.6) deepsetScompAtt-plain-ftheadexpand-medshift-260827 0.069 0.157
ref: hand-token itemScompAtt (§14.2) itemScompAtt-ftheadexpand-260809 0.139 0.119
ref: pretrained encoder \(+\) Ukraine head fine-tune deepsetScompAtt-itemamortise-J64-ukraineft-260831 0.133 —

Association with the SVI target (Pearson \(\rho\) of the amortiser median against the SVI draw, small–mid \(n\)): \(0.91\)–\(0.95\) on the out-of-7 items and \(0.73\)–\(0.76\) on the categorical items, against the item-specific baseline’s \(\approx0.82/0.57\). The scale-feat row is the deployable per-family network; the two references above and below it bracket the recipe-bound floor discussed next.

A recipe-bound floor. The residual conditional gap to the item-specific \(0.069\) is set by the recipe, not by item generality: a plain quantile head deploys at \(\approx0.112\) and per-item \(\hat\sigma_j\) reaches \(0.069\) (§14.4.9), both of which the item-amortised net gives up (a global \(\hat\sigma\), a power-law head). Transferring the pretrained encoder to Ukraine and fine-tuning does not move the deployed PIT–KS (\(0.133\)) while that recipe is fixed — the head type and scale, not the encoder, are binding. Closing the last of the gap therefore means reverting to a per-item-set network.

Deployment scope — idiosyncratic items. Because the network prices an item from its features, not its identity, an item whose behaviour those features do not capture is priced from the wrong prior. Ukraine’s degenerate CG-VIO_ph-punish — the blow item excluded from every contraction fit (§14.4.5) — is the clear case: in the cross-method \(p(H_1\mid x,z)\) comparison (§14.7) the item-general net saturates at \(p(H_1)\approx1\) from interim 3 on (\(\eta_0=0.5\); medians \(0.87,1.0,1.0,\dots\)), while the two Ukraine-bespoke encoders — itemScompAtt and the plain deepset, which saw this item in training — track the HMC gold standard (\(\approx0.2\)–\(0.5\)). This is the deployment-scope trade-off made concrete: the per-family net generalises across item sets but is weaker on an individual idiosyncratic or boundary item than a net trained on the specific instrument. Expected behaviour, not a defect.

Conclusion. One network prices any partial-credit item set of size \(2\)–\(64\) from item features alone, deploying at PIT–KS \(0.119\) / marg–KS \(0.159\) on Ukraine — conditionally sharper than the hand-token encoder and marginally on par with the bespoke deepset. Deploying on a new application is then: feed its items, metadata and cohorts, and run the head fine-tune \(+\) affine (and BvM) against that application’s own SVI. The §14.4 amortiser becomes a per-family asset across the §3 datasets, at a small, recipe-bound calibration cost relative to a bespoke network.

14.4.11 A net registry indexed by endpoint functional, and whether one token suffices

§14.4.10 showed one network prices any item set. The remaining organising question is not the items but the endpoint functional \(\rho\): a seroprotection rate \(P(y\ge k)\), a GMT fold-rise, a signed relative change, a between-group difference. Two structural choices depend on the functional — the per-token summary (a scalar normalised mean, or the wide cumulative-exceedance vector \([\,\mathbf 1\{k\ge c\}\,]_c\) that is caseness-sufficient, §14.4.18) and the deploy warp (none / \(\log_2\) / logit) — while the item family does not (the Ukraine-trained encoder deploys on the flu titre family at PIT–KS \(\approx0.08\), §3.18). This motivates a net registry keyed by functional, not item family. A registry instance is one item-general §14.4.10 network (deep-set over participants, item self-attention, quantile head), trained for a single endpoint functional; it is identified by the pair \((\text{reduction},\text{compare})\) that defines \(\rho\), which in turn fixes the two structural deploy choices — the token (scalar mean vs wide cumulative-exceedance) and the warp (none / \(\log_2\) / logit). It is not keyed by item family: one instance prices any item set within the §14.4.10 envelope (\(\le 64\) items, \(\le 10\) levels). The current registry:

instance functional \(\rho\) reduction / compare token warp example \(H_1\) applications (manifest) status
\(S_1\) protection rate \(P(y\ge k)\) threshold / level wide logit SPR \(>0.70\) flu SDY312/314, head-to-head (per arm) trained
\(S_2\) GMT fold-rise mean / \(\mathrm{fold}_{\log_2}\) scalar \(\log_2\) GMFR \(>2.5\) flu, head-to-head (per arm) trained
\(S_3\) relative change, mean (signed) mean / ratio scalar none \(>0\) / effect size ICRC, MYCELIUM, REFUGE, CAVD, Ukraine (out-of-7) trained
\(S_4\) relative change, caseness threshold / ratio wide none \(>0\) Ukraine (categorical) trained (§14.4.11)
\(S_5\) between-group difference (standardised) any / diff wide none \(>0\) head-to-head (LAIV\(-\)TIV) to train
\(S_6\) per-participant seroconversion ($$4-fold) paired rate wide + paired sim logit SCR \(>0.40\) flu composite (SCR \(\vee\) SPR \(\vee\) GMFR) later

An application’s manifest is just the list of instances it deploys: flu \(\{S_1,S_2\}\); head-to-head \(\{S_1,S_2\;\text{per arm},\,S_5\;\text{difference}\}\); the psychometric datasets \(\{S_3\}\); Ukraine \(\{S_3\;\text{(out-of-7)},\,S_4\;\text{(categorical caseness)}\}\). Deployment is uniform (§14.4.6–14.4.7): filter the SVI reference to that endpoint (one pps_ratio_x), fit the expanding head \(+\) affine, apply the head BvM, read the PPS in natural units after unwarping. New endpoints or datasets add a row or a manifest entry, never a new architecture.

Because the wide token is sufficient — its mean-pool is the empirical category CDF, carrying the mean, any threshold, and group contrasts — it is natural to ask whether the registry collapses further: one widetok encoder for all functionals, the functional selected only by the deploy-time head fine-tune. We test this on Ukraine as an amortiser-methods comparison (the Ukraine data question of §14 is irrelevant here — we compare networks against the same fixed SVI reference; no new fit). Three encoders are deployed on the identical Ukraine relative-change reference (weekly SVI, 20 items, 29 interims \(n=48\!\to\!500\)) under identical settings — warp none, head BvM on, expanding head fine-tune, \(N_{\mathrm{ref}}=503\) — so only the encoder \(\times\) token differ: the scalar scale-feat net (the current default for \(S_3\)), the caseness-dedicated widetok-SPR net, and a purpose-trained widetok-MIXED net whose target is per item the relative change (typ \(0\)) or the endline rate (typ \(1\)), \(\approx\) half each, forcing one encoder to carry both.

Metrics (both against the SVI reference). PIT–KS — conditional calibration: for each (interim, item) form the probability-integral transform \(u=\hat F(\rho^{\mathrm{svi}})\) of the amortiser’s posterior CDF at the draw-aligned SVI value; PIT–KS is the Kolmogorov–Smirnov distance of the pooled \(u\) from \(\mathrm{Unif}[0,1]\) (a well-calibrated conditional posterior gives uniform \(u\)). marg–KS — marginal calibration: the KS distance between the SVI marginal CDF and the amortiser mixture CDF on a shared grid. Coverage cov5/cov95 are the empirical masses below the nominal \(0.05\)/\(0.95\) quantiles.

Fair aggregation. One Ukraine item, CG-VIO_ph-punish, is a known functional pathology — a signed relative change with \(\approx 25\%\) of its posterior mass negative, unstable near that boundary (the same instability that made the head-to-head difference, not percent, the sane cross-arm estimand, §3.21) — and it saturates the scalar/mean deploy (PIT–KS \(0.84\), §14.4.10). Since it is present at every interim it inflates every aggregate, so it is dropped throughout — from the per-item means and the per-interim (across-\(n\)) aggregate (RAGD_EXCLUDE_ITEMS) — leaving 19 items.

PIT–KS is by cohort-size regime (per-interim, CG-VIO excluded); marg–KS and mean posterior width \(W\) are over all interims. The last row is the federated deploy — scalar \(S_3\) on the 15 out-of-7 items, dedicated caseness \(S_4\) (next paragraph) on the 4 categorical — the alternative to a single encoder.

approach token PIT–KS \(n\le200\) PIT–KS mid PIT–KS \(n\ge450\) PIT–KS all marg–KS width \(W\)
scalar (\(S_3\) default) scalar 0.073 0.084 0.096 0.088 0.110 0.43
widetok-SPR (rate, wrong target) wide 0.157 0.150 0.104 0.129 0.298 0.21
widetok-MIXED (single encoder) wide 0.079 0.092 0.101 0.094 0.123 0.34
FEDERATED (\(S_3\) mean \(+\) \(S_4\) caseness) scalar \(+\) wide 0.070 0.080 0.092 0.084 0.110 0.46

(bins: \(n\le200\) 6 interims, mid 9, \(n\ge450\) 14; cov95 \(\approx0.91\)–\(0.94\) except widetok-SPR \(\approx0.85\) at mid; \(W\) = mean marginal width, widetok-SPR narrowest \(=\) over-confident. The per-item PIT–KS aggregate gives the same ordering.)

For a stopping rule the small-\(n\) end carries the decision, and the table’s regime split is the point: scalar, mixed and federated are sharpest and best-covered there, while widetok-SPR — a rate-dedicated net on a mean reference — is worst early and only competitive late, exactly wrong for early stopping.

Three findings. (i) The wide token is not the bottleneck: on the matched mixed target the widetok encoder recovers to near-scalar, so the widetok-SPR deficit was target mismatch — the single-encoder collapse is feasible. (ii) But it does not dominate: the dedicated scalar stays marginally sharper (a small generality tax) and less over-confident. (iii) The pathology is functional, not architectural: CG-VIO_ph-punish is rescued only when scored as a rate/caseness rather than a signed ratio (\(0.84\!\to\!0.17\)), which no encoder change achieves — the estimand does.

Federated per-functional vs the single encoder. Completing the registry, we trained the missing net \(S_4\) — a widetok encoder for the categorical items’ caseness relative change (widetok-SPR carries the endline rate, the wrong compare) — and deployed it on the 4 categorical items with the scalar \(S_3\) on the 15 out-of-7. The federated set is the sharpest (table); on every categorical item the dedicated caseness net edges the mean net, so even the mono scalar was slightly overpaying. The single mixed encoder trails by \(\approx0.03\) PIT–KS — one asset over several.

Conclusion. Adopt the registry as \(S_1\)–\(S_6\) keyed by functional, no item-family axis: scalar token for mean-type (\(S_2\)/\(S_3\)), wide for rate/difference (\(S_1\)/\(S_4\)/\(S_5\)). The federated per-functional set is marginally the sharpest; the single-widetok collapse is a proven fallback at a small (\(\approx0.02\)–\(0.03\) PIT–KS) tax. And an item that miscalibrates because its functional is unstable is fixed by the estimand (a difference or rate), not the network.

14.5 What the deep-set summary sees: the shuffle test

14.5.1 Cross-item correlation blindness, confirmed on the Ukraine data

The §14.4 encoder embeds each (participant, item) independently — \(q_{\text{input}}\) reads item \(j\)’s own \((\text{baseline}, \text{endline})\) token, never another item’s — and mean-pools over participants per item. The whole representation (\(e(x), e(z)\), keys \(k\), attention output \(h\), head) is therefore a function of the per-item (bivariate) marginal empirical distributions of \(x\) and \(z\): it carries no cross-item (joint) information. The §14.1 empirical \(\hat K\)-row — the one hand-added item–item-correlation channel — was dropped (§14.4.1), so the deep-set cannot see how items co-vary across participants. (It does keep more than a group mean: the full per-item marginal, including each item’s own baseline↔︎endline relationship, which the endpoint \(\rho\) needs.)

This is testable directly, by two marginal-preserving permutations of the real Ukraine cohorts (both \(x\) and \(z^{(s)}\)):

  • cross-item — permute the participant axis independently per item, keeping each participant’s \((\text{baseline}, \text{endline})\) pair for that item intact. This leaves every item’s marginal exactly unchanged and destroys the cross-item joint. Because each per-item pool is a sum over the same set of per-participant embeddings, the output must be invariant — iff the net is correlation-blind.
  • within-item (control) — permute baseline and endline independently per item. This breaks each item’s own baseline↔︎endline joint, a change the net can see, confirming it is not simply dead.

Over early, mid and late interims (\(S = 100\) draws each; the deepsetScompAtt plain net, \(J = 20\)):

interim \(k\) \(n\) \(m\) cross-item \(\max\lvert\Delta\hat\rho\rvert\) cross-item \(\max\lvert\Delta\text{PPS}\rvert\) within-item \(\max\lvert\Delta\hat\rho\rvert\) within-item \(\max\lvert\Delta\text{PPS}\rvert\)
2 48 455 \(1.3\times10^{-6}\) \(0\) \(0.015\) \(0.010\)
15 416 87 \(1.4\times10^{-6}\) \(0\) \(0.007\) \(0.000\)
29 498 5 \(1.5\times10^{-6}\) \(0\) \(0.037\) \(0.010\)

The cross-item shuffle moves the predictive quantiles by \(\sim 10^{-6}\) — float32 summation-order noise in the segmented mean — and the PPS decision by exactly zero: the deep-set is exactly correlation-blind across items, confirmed on real data at every cohort size. The within-item control moves \(\hat\rho\) by \(0.007\)–\(0.037\) (and the occasional PPS flip), so the net genuinely uses each item’s own marginal; the cross-item invariance is a real property, not a dead network.

Takeaway. The deep-set reaches for each item’s full marginal, not for item–item covariance — so dropping the \(\hat K\)-row (§14.4.1) is a genuine information loss, not a free simplification. Recovering the covariance channel needs cross-item mixing before the pool, not the current pool-then-attend: either a 2nd-order pooled statistic (pairwise products = §14.1’s \(\hat K\)-row revived) or per-participant self-attention. The options, and a concrete batched-attention design, are §14.6; whether the channel pays is the open A/B against §14.1’s K-row. Script: scripts-py/Ukraine_deepset_shuffle_test.py.

Across applications: how much covariance is there to discard? The cross-item blindness is architectural — a mean-pool of per-item-independent embeddings is marginal-only on any data — so the shuffle test’s cross-item invariance holds for every application regardless of how correlated its items are. What varies across applications is how much genuine covariance the pool throws away. Measuring the raw cross-item correlation (participant × item, within one condition, mean \(|r|\) over item pairs) ranks them:

application items \(J\) mean $ r
REFUGE-ED (MSPSS social support) 12 0.52 0.98
ICRC-DASS-DRC (depression/anxiety/stress) 3 0.53 1.00
Ukraine (psychometric) 20 0.26 0.33
HVTN505 BAMA (antibody binding) 9 0.22 0.25

REFUGE-ED stands out — a 12-item scale with three correlated sub-scales (Family / Friends / Significant-Other), \(\sim 2\times\) Ukraine’s item correlation and almost every pair above \(0.3\) (ICRC-DASS is equally correlated but only \(J=3\), little room for cross-item machinery). Running the shuffle test on REFUGE-ED (the item-general J64 deep-set applied to the MSPSS cohorts, \(S=100\)) nonetheless gives the same result as Ukraine, exactly as the architecture dictates:

interim \(k\) \(n\) \(m\) cross-item \(\max\lvert\Delta\hat\rho\rvert\) cross-item \(\max\lvert\Delta\text{PPS}\rvert\) within-item \(\max\lvert\Delta\hat\rho\rvert\) within-item \(\max\lvert\Delta\text{PPS}\rvert\)
2 81 243 \(1.3\times10^{-6}\) \(0\) \(0.006\) \(0\)
5 202 122 \(1.2\times10^{-6}\) \(0\) \(0.008\) \(0\)
8 324 1 \(1.8\times10^{-6}\) \(0\) \(0.006\) \(0\)

Cross-item shuffle moves the predictive quantiles by float noise and the PPS by exactly zero — on REFUGE the deep-set therefore discards substantially more real cross-item structure than on Ukraine, yet the blindness is identical. One caveat caps the upside of recovering it anywhere: the per-item endpoint \(\rho_j\) is a functional of item \(j\)’s marginal (relative improvement, SPR, GMFR), so cross-item covariance can never change the target — it can only sharpen the inference (borrow strength to tighten \(\hat\rho_j\)). On Ukraine that inference gain was nil (Option B did not pay, §14.6.2). REFUGE-ED is the prime remaining test-bed because it has the most covariance to exploit, but the marginal-endpoint argument bounds how much even an ideal covariance channel can buy; the Option B A/B on REFUGE is the open experiment. Script: scripts-py/Refuge_deepset_shuffle_test.py.

14.5.2 Where the query reads from: item embedding vs. key

The attention query can read either the item embedding \(e^{j, s} = [e^j(x), e^{j, s}(z)] \in \mathbb{R}^{2E}\) (the concatenated cohort embeddings) or the key \(k^{j, s} = q_{\text{keys}}(e^{j, s}) \in \mathbb{R}^{E}\). The original build queried the key, \(q^{j, s} = q_{\text{query}}(k^{j, s})\), which is slightly unusual — a query is normally a separate projection of its own source, and here it instead shares the \(q_{\text{keys}}\) representation. The conceptually cleaner choice reads the item embedding, \(q^{j, s} = q_{\text{query}}(e^{j, s})\) (input dimension \(2E\)), making query and key independent reads of \(e^{j, s}\), as in §14.1.4. Both were trained from the same prior-predictive stream and deployed identically (weekly SVI reference, head-ft-expand, no BvM):

\(q_{\text{query}}\) input cov\(_5\) cov\(_{95}\) PIT-KS marg-KS
item embedding \(e^{j, s}\) (\(2E\)) default 0.55 0.96 0.106 0.159
key \(k^{j, s}\) (\(E\)) legacy (query_from_token=False) 0.52 0.94 0.078 0.188

It is a calibration wash: the embedding-query trades slightly worse PIT-KS (\(0.106\) vs \(0.078\)) for slightly better marginal-KS (\(0.159\) vs \(0.188\)) and marginally tighter tail coverage, no consistent winner, PPS decisions essentially unchanged. The query source is not calibration-critical — the attention scores depend on it only through a softmax over \(J = 20\) keys, and the head keeps a direct skip to the queried item’s own key \(k^{j^*, s}\) regardless. Since calibration does not decide it, we default to the embedding-query (query_from_token=True) on the cleaner-architecture argument; the key-query variant stays reachable via the flag, and pre-flag checkpoints are pinned to it in load_fitted_model (their \(q_{\text{query}}\) was trained at input \(E\)). Trainer: PP_QFT (default 1) in scripts-py/Ukraine_interim_analysis_amortise_endpt_deepsetScompAtt_ragged_priorproposal_explore.py.

14.5.3 Separate value projection

The original §14.4.4 tied the attention values to the keys, \(v^{j, s} = k^{j, s}\): the same embedding both scores (as a key) and is averaged (as a value). A standard transformer keeps a separate \(W_V \neq W_K\), so the mix can live in a different feature space than the scoring. We tested the untied form \(v^{j, s} = q_{\text{values}}(e^{j, s})\) (a third MLP, input \(2E\)), attention output \(h^{j^*, s} = \sum_j \omega^{j^*, s}_j\, v^{j, s}\), trained from the same prior-predictive stream and deployed identically (token-query, weekly SVI reference, head-ft-expand, no BvM):

values cov\(_5\) cov\(_{95}\) PIT-KS marg-KS
separate \(v^{j,s} = q_{\text{values}}(e^{j,s})\) default 0.51 0.96 0.069 0.150
tied \(v^{j,s} = k^{j,s}\) legacy (separate_values=False) 0.55 0.96 0.106 0.159

Unlike the query-source wash (§14.5.2), the separate value projection is a small but consistent win across all four metrics: PIT-KS \(0.106 \to 0.069\), marg-KS \(0.159 \to 0.150\), and \(\text{cov}_5\) \(0.55 \to 0.51\) (essentially nominal), \(\text{cov}_{95}\) unchanged. Freeing the averaged context from the scoring representation measurably sharpens the conditional. It costs one extra \(2E\to E\to E\to E\) MLP (\(\sim 2{,}400\) params, §14.4.4). On the strength of this we adopt it as the default (separate_values=True, PP_SEPV=1); the legacy tied form stays reachable via the flag, and pre-flag checkpoints are pinned to it in load_fitted_model. Flag in the same trainer as §14.5.2.

14.6 Toward a correlation-aware deep-set

The shuffle test (§14.5.1) showed the §14.4 encoder is exactly blind to item–item covariance: a mean-pool of per-item-independent embeddings is a function of the per-item marginals only. The cause is structural — a mean is linear and item-separable, so any cross-item information present in a participant’s response vector is destroyed the moment we pool. The only cure is to inject cross-item coupling before the participant pool (the existing §14.4.4 item self-attention sits after the pool, over already-marginal item embeddings, so it cannot recover what the pool threw away). Two routes:

  • Option A — a 2nd-order pooled statistic (pairwise products). Augment each participant’s features with the cross-item products \(t^{j}_{i}\,t^{j'}_{i}\) and pool; \(\frac1n\sum_i t_i t_i^\top\) is the empirical item Gram/covariance matrix. This is exactly §14.1’s hand \(\hat K\)-row revived as a pooled sufficient statistic. It is exact at 2nd order, deterministic and interpretable, but captures only linear correlation, hand-fixes the bilinear basis, and costs \(O(J^2)\) features — for ragged \(J\) the per-participant pair vector is variable-length and must itself be set-pooled, so it does not escape a set architecture over items.
  • Option B — per-participant self-attention before the pool. Let each participant’s \(J\) item embeddings attend to one another, then pool. This learns which cross-item interactions matter (any order, not a fixed 2nd-moment basis), is natively permutation-equivariant in \(J\) (no fixed-size feature vector), and — because \(J\) is small and fixed — is a cheap batched dense attention over the participant axis (no flash attention needed; §14.6.1). Option B subsumes Option A (one attention layer can represent 2nd-moment mixing and more), so we detail it; Option A remains worth building as a cheap, interpretable 2nd-order baseline in the A/B.

14.6.1 Option B: per-participant self-attention before the pool

Where it goes. Insert a shared self-attention block \(\mathcal S\) between the per-item embedding and the participant pool of §14.4.4’s Stage 1. Keep the §14.4.4 tokens \(t^{j}_{i}(x) = y_{i,j}\), \(t^{j,s}_{i}(z) = z^{s}_{i,j} \in \mathbb{R}^{R}\) and the per-item embedding \(e_{i,j} = q_{\text{input}}(t^{j}_{i}, M_j) \in \mathbb{R}^{E}\), but do not pool yet: collect participant \(i\)’s \(J\) item embeddings into a matrix and mix them.

The block (one single-head SAB; Vaswani et al. 2017, Lee et al. 2019). Single-head throughout, matching §14.4.4 (the whole architecture is deliberately single-head and lean). For interim participant \(i\), stack \(E_i(x) = [\,e_{i,1};\dots;e_{i,J}\,] \in \mathbb{R}^{J\times E}\) and self-attend over its \(J\) items with linear projections \(W_Q, W_K, W_V, W_O \in \mathbb{R}^{E\times E}\) shared across all participants and both cohorts (so participant exchangeability is preserved): \[Q_i = E_i W_Q,\;\; K_i = E_i W_K,\;\; V_i = E_i W_V \in \mathbb{R}^{J\times E}, \qquad A_i = \operatorname{softmax}\!\Big(\tfrac{Q_i K_i^\top}{\sqrt E}\Big) \in \mathbb{R}^{J\times J},\] \[\widetilde E_i = E_i + (A_i V_i)\,W_O \in \mathbb{R}^{J\times E} \quad(\text{residual; optional position-wise FFN}),\] giving cross-item-aware per-participant embeddings \(\tilde e_{i,j} \in \mathbb{R}^{E}\) (row \(j\) of \(\widetilde E_i\)). The identical block runs on each future participant’s \(E_i^{s}(z)\) per draw \(s\). Only then pool over participants, exactly as §14.4.4: \[e^{j}(x) = \tfrac1n\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde e_{i,j} \in \mathbb{R}^{E}, \qquad e^{j,s}(z) = \tfrac1m\sum_{i=n+1}^{N}\tilde e^{\,s}_{i,j} \in \mathbb{R}^{E}.\] Stage 2 onward (item embedding \(e^{j,s}=[e^j(x),e^{j,s}(z)]\), keys/query/values, item self-attention, head) is unchanged.

Dimensions.

object shape axis
token \(t^{j}_{i}\) \(\mathbb{R}^{R}\), \(R=2\) per (participant, item)
item embedding \(e_{i,j}\) \(\mathbb{R}^{E}\), \(E=32\) per (participant, item)
per-participant stack \(E_i\) \(\mathbb{R}^{J\times E}\) per participant
attention scores \(A_i\) \(\mathbb{R}^{J\times J}\) (single head) per participant
mixed embedding \(\widetilde E_i\) \(\mathbb{R}^{J\times E}\) per participant
pooled item embedding \(e^{j}(x), e^{j,s}(z)\) \(\mathbb{R}^{E}\) per item (per draw)

Added parameters: \(\approx 4E^2\) per block (\(W_{Q,K,V,O}\); \(+\) FFN if used) \(\approx 4{,}100\), on the §14.4.4 scale.

Implementation — batched dense attention, not flash. Despite the earlier framing, flash attention is the wrong tool here: \(J\) is small and fixed (\(=20\)), so each \(J\times J\) score block is \(\sim\!400\) entries and materialises trivially. The large axis is not the sequence \(J\) but the batch of independent tiny attentions, \(B' = n + mS\) participant-instances (\(n\) from \(x\), \(m\) per draw \(\times S\) from \(z\)). The efficient form is one batched dense attention (single head) vectorised over \(B'\): \[E_{\text{all}} \in \mathbb{R}^{B'\times J\times E}, \qquad \text{scores} = \operatorname{einsum}('bje,bke\to bjk') \in \mathbb{R}^{B'\times J\times J}, \qquad \widetilde E = \operatorname{einsum}('bjk,bke\to bje'),\] softmax over the last axis.
